RBC Capital Markets
2
Higher Ground, Harder Climb – Our Thoughts On the Year-Ahead Outlook For US Equities
In a Nutshell
In Pulse we tackle hot topics and updates on our key indicators. This week, a special edition discussing our thoughts on the year-ahead outlook. The big things you need to know: First, our 12-month 
forward S&P 500 target is 8,050, which represents a 16.5% gain from the 11/20/26 close. Bearish investor sentiment, a solid GDP backdrop, and strong corporate earnings argue for a path higher, 
offsetting the additional P/E compression that we expect to see. Second, we run through our thoughts on how much more downside may be left in the current drawdown from a historical perspective and in 
terms of what we’re seeing in some of our bottom-up valuation and sentiment indicators. Third, while the Large Cap Growth trade may outperform in the short term once the broader market stabilizes, on a 
12-month view we prefer Value over Growth and Small over Large. For sectors, we remain overweight Financials and Health Care in the S&P 500, and on the sidelines with Tech where we are market 
weight.  
Our 12-Month S&P 500 Price Target Is 8,050
With the S&P 500 down 11.6% from its early October high as of Friday’s close, it is an admittedly tricky time to issue a year-ahead outlook. But in some ways, it is a good time to do so. Debating a potential 
bottom and formulating year ahead projections both require careful attention to data.  While we may adjust some of our thinking based on how things play out through the end of the year, we think this 
exercise helps us stay focused on the longer term.  Overall, what our work is telling us today about the 12-month view is that a path higher for stocks remains in place, despite the recent volatility.   
Our 12-month price target of 8,050 is an approximation of the output of our revamped valuation/EPS model, one of five quantitative models that we regularly consult when formulating our 12-month view. 
This model projects an LTM P/E for a specific point in time (in this case 4Q27) based on assumptions for interest rates, EPS growth and inflation, taking into account both levels and rates of change 
(adding rates of change is one of several methodological adjustments we’ve made to this model for the new year). Leveraging RBC house views for macro indicators and bottom-up consensus forecasts 
for EPS, the model calls for some further compression in the LTM P/E to 18.8x in 4Q27. Even though the deceleration in EPS growth that’s embedded in the consensus EPS forecasts puts some 
downward pressure on the P/E in our modeling, the dollar value of EPS growth is still strong enough to point to a fair value estimate of 8,050 for YE2027. See page 7.
The other four models we often use in our price target process allow us to look at the path higher for equities from other perspectives. They are based on sentiment, cross asset dynamics, and the Fed and 
GDP backdrops. See page 6 for a full rundown.  These are return based models which use a recent close as their starting point.  We’re leaving them out of our target for now given the sharp slide seen 
over the past week in the S&P 500, but their signals are still helpful in thinking through the potential path for stocks.  
The most constructive of these is our AAII sentiment indicator, which is contrarian in nature. Net bullishness has fallen enough on that model (to -11.85% on a 4-week average in the latest update) that its 
current reading tends to be followed by a 12-month forward return of +15.1% in the S&P 500. In other words, a lot of bad news has already gotten baked in heading into the new year.  See page 15.
Another noteworthy model is our economic test, which reminds us that the S&P 500 tends to rise about 10% on a 12-month time frame when real GDP ends up in the 2.1-3% range. Both RBC Economics 
and consensus economic forecasts are near the low end of this range. If AI or Iran war related issues trigger a softening of GDP views, it’s worth remembering that the S&P 500 still tends to rise 6.3% on 
average when real GDP is in the 1.1-2% range. It’s not until GDP growth ends up in the 0.1-1% range that the S&P 500 tends to be down. See page 33.
Beyond the possibility that GDP and EPS come in cooler than expected, we see monetary policy and interest rates as the biggest risk to our expectation that stocks can be meaningfully higher a year from 
now. Our cross-asset model (a simple earnings yield gap) is still in a favorable range for stocks (the modest negative gap is consistent with a 13.4% 12-month forward return, page 23). But if 10-year yields 
move above 6%, (and the EPS yield stays constant) this model would flip into a range that has typically been accompanied by negative 12-month forward returns for the S&P 500. Similarly, our Fed test 
tells us that when the Fed hikes no more than 100 bps in a 12-month time frame, the S&P 500 tends to rise by 13.7% (page 44). But if cumulative hikes in a 12-month period amount to more than 100 bps 
and up to 200 bps, the typical forward return drops to 7.4%. While the multifaceted “Tech wreck” that’s occurred in November has dominated headlines, we’ve been more rattled by comments from Fed 
officials that have alluded to the possibility of bigger hikes. Since the mid 1990’s, when the Fed moves gradually with mostly 25 basis point hikes, the S&P 500 has tended to sell off at the beginning of the 
hiking cycle and then move higher over the rest of it. But when we get mostly chunky hikes (with an average bps per move in the upper 40s, i.e. 1994-1995 and 2022), the S&P 500 tends to have a tougher 
time getting back on a path higher (pages 45-46). 
Overall, the story all of our quant models tell us is that stocks have already priced in a significant amount of bad news, and a strong economic and earnings backdrop should be able to offset the pressures 
of a bit more P/E contraction and a few more Fed hikes. Our modeling also helps us understand what the implications could be if these assumptions end up being a bit too optimistic. Separately, when we 
do a more qualitative review of the different drivers of US equity market performance (pages 8-9), the story we see is one of fading positives and emerging concerns. Strength in the EPS and GDP 
backdrop as well as capex are under scrutiny due to doubts swirling around the AI narrative. The short, modest, and manageable hiking cycle investors have been expecting has also seemed at risk of 
turning into something bigger and longer. We suspect the AI debate will be with us for quite some time and won’t be fully resolved soon, keeping markets edgy into the early part of next year.  

RBC Capital Markets
3
Higher Ground, Harder Climb – Our Thoughts On the Year-Ahead Outlook For US Equities (continued)
Recent Downside In Context
As of Friday’s close, the 11.6% drawdown in the S&P 500 was a little worse than the Tier 1 garden variety pullback (5-10%) that we’ve been worried about since mid September and the losses seen after the Iran war began 
(page 11). But the index wasn’t quite back down into Tier 2 growth scare territory (14-20%) on our Tiers of Fear framework (page 10). 
It’s not clear to us whether the S&P 500 has put a bottom in place. We’ve spent a lot of time studying the history of drawdowns. Looking at it purely from a numerical perspective, an argument can be made both ways. Recent 
history suggests there may be more pain to come. If we use 2009 as the starting point, there simply haven’t been any drawdowns in the 11-12% range in the post GFC era. When drawdowns hit the 10% mark and stocks don’t 
stabilize there tends to be an air pocket with drawdowns going to 14% or beyond. In total there have been five Tier 2 growth scares with 14-20% drawdowns since the GFC. These tend to involve a new challenge that hits the 
stock market, creating a crisis of confidence and, in most cases, genuine fear of a recession that doesn’t materialize (the European debt crisis of 2010, the US debt downgrade of 2011, the 2015-2016 industrial recession, 
2018’s perfect storm of tariffs / Fed anxiety / midterms, and 2025’s liberation day tariffs). 
If we take a longer look back (page 12), we also find that drawdowns in the 11-12% range are rare.  We did identify two, however, in the late 1990’s that bottomed out in the 11-12% area, which provides some precedent for the 
S&P 500 to bottom soon, if it hasn’t already.  One of these was a 10.8% drawdown in the fall of 1997 around the Asian Financial Crisis. The other was a 12.1% drawdown in late 1999, right around when the Fed started a new 
hiking cycle. In addition to worries about inflation and hikes, Y2K anxiety was also weighing on investors.  While it’s not a perfect comparison with today given the company specific issues in focus now, the Fed and 
cyber/outage concerns of the past few weeks contain some echoes. 
Beyond price action, what else are we watching? Stats-wise, valuation and sentiment. In our November 9th Pulse, we noted that the bottom-up forward P/Es for the S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 got to levels on November 5th
where the broader market had tended to fight back since early 2025, but were not back down to 2022 lows (pages 21 & 112). For example, the S&P 500 bottom-up market cap weighted NTM P/E hit 20.5x on November 5th, 
similar to the Iran war and 2025 tariff lows of 21.6x and 21.2x, but not close to the 2022 low of 18.6x. Additionally, while decently below average at 14.6x, the Russell 2000 market cap weighted FY2 P/E remained well above the 
11-13x range that tends to signal capitulation in major drawdowns (page 172). Similarly, we noted that AAII net bulls had retreated to post 2025 lows right after the 2026 midterms (around -20%) but were not yet back to 2022 
and 2025 lows (around -40%, page 15). In other words, there had been enough pain to permit a bottom, but not enough to say conditions were clearly oversold. That’s still what we were seeing on these bottom-up forward 
P/E’s as of Thursday’s close (at the time of writing we did not have Friday’s update). AAII net bulls improved a little last week. We will be keeping a close eye on these charts going forward. 
Narrative-wise, we’re keeping an eye on the tone of the discussion around the economy, earnings, and labor. Looking back to the mid 1950’s, major drawdowns of 11% or more often involve outright contraction in corporate 
profits growth and major downward EPS estimate revisions, negative GDP or growth rates that come close, and breakdowns in labor market indicators. The current environment is puzzling, and things could change, but right 
now we’re not seeing a case being made for those kinds of conditions which suggests to us the current drawdown should not go beyond tier 2 (in the post GFC era, tier 2 declines have ended up being shocks caused by 
unusual events or recession near misses). Admittedly, an interest rate shock heavily contributed to 2022’s 25% “tier 3” drawdown, keeping that issue on our radar as well. 
More Interest In Value, Cyclicals, Defensives, and Small Caps Than Large Cap Growth  In The Year Ahead
While we acknowledge that Large Cap Growth and Tech may initially bounce hardest after the broader market stabilizes, for the next 12-months we generally favor rotation trades. 
▪
Our Growth/Value indicators are mixed, but we see an edge for Value.  Per our scorecard (page 104), there are three main arguments in favor of Value: 1) Positioning – Growth still looks crowded per CFTC’s data on 
Nasdaq futures positioning (page 109), 2) The economic resilience of the US -- Value tends to outperform when US GDP expectations are rising (page 120), and 3) Political winds – while it’s unclear what can and will 
actually happen in Washington, any expression of the will of US voters seems to carry tailwinds for cyclicals (a desire to end the Iran war) and headwinds for the secular Growth AI theme (anti AI/data center angst, views on 
corruption and linkages to big Tech in campaign messaging). The main argument in favor of Growth is more attractive valuations (page 114) and a better earnings backdrop, but our work shows a reduction of earnings 
dominance for Growth from a few angles (pages 116 & 118). Interestingly, Value has tended to hold up better recently when stock market volatility spikes and financial conditions deteriorate (pages 110-111). On a related 
note, while our work is not giving a clear signal on the US/non-US developed markets trade, we have found that it tends to track the US Growth/Value trade (see page 78 for further thoughts on the US in a global context).  
▪
On S&P 500 sectors, we remain overweight Financials & Health Care. A full rundown of our sector views is on page 135, with more details on Financials on page 140 and Health Care on page 141. Both sectors have 
outperformed since the Tech trade peaked in early October but haven’t been at the top of the rankings (page 134). In both sectors, we see reasonable absolute valuations, slightly attractive relative valuations, solid EPS 
revisions trends, and positive/improving funds flows in recent weeks/months. Both sectors also tend to outperform when consumer confidence is rising, though the signal is stronger for Health Care. We remain on the 
sidelines with Tech (page 143), which we downgraded from overweight to market weight on October 14th. Tech valuations look attractive again and earnings revisions are still positive. But we are paying more attention to 
the direction of travel. Valuations for the broader sector are clearly contracting but have not hit important lows of the past. Deterioration is also seen for the rate of upward EPS estimate revisions for the broader Tech sector 
and Semis in particular, which seems early innings (page 212). 
▪
We are sticking with Small Caps, as the positives we see outweigh the negatives. While Fed hikes are usually more of a problem for Small Caps, we see four reasons to maintain a positive bias on Small Caps 
relative to Large Caps. These are 1) Positioning – Nasdaq 100 futures still look over owned on CFTC’s data, while Russell 2000 futures still look under owned but with trends in place that suggest investors are getting less 
negative (page 168). 2) Valuations – the Russell 2000 has broken below its long-term average on forward P/E (page 172), something we’ve not seen yet for the S&P 500. 3) The economy – Small Caps tend to outperform 
when GDP forecasts are rising, jobs growth is accelerating, and ISM manufacturing is rising. We are mindful that these indicators may weaken but it’s not what we’re seeing on the data right now (pages181, 183 & 185). 4) 
Government policy – if Congress were to act on its affordability agenda, we think this would help Small Caps more given their more cyclical nature. AI/datacenter backlash is also something more likely to impact the S&P 
500 than the Russell 2000. Small Caps admittedly tend to suffer more when rate hikes are being added to the forecast, but we are mindful that they underperformed from late June through mid October in large part because 
of rising investor concerns on this issue (page 186).

The Models We’re Watching As We Think About The Outlook
S&P 500 Outlook / Price Target 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy; priced 11/20/2026
▪
Our 12-month price target for the S&P 500 is 8,050. This is slightly lower than our September update, where our 12-month target was 8,150. 
Note, we view our price target as a compass, not a GPS, and a tool designed to articulate whether stocks can go higher and why. 
▪
We often prefer to use the median or average of five models which are based on sentiment, valuation/EPS, cross asset analysis, the economic 
backdrop, and the interest rate backdrop. Since four of the five are return based and use a close price to calculate their estimated fair value for 
the index, and the stock market has been falling rapidly in recent days, we have decided in this update to rely on the model that does not have 
the latest close price as a variable. 
▪
The model we are leveraging for this price target is an updated version of our valuation/EPS model, which provides the most optimistic 
projection. It bakes in the idea of further P/E compression alongside strong but decelerating EPS growth in the year ahead. This model produces 
a fair value estimate of 8,063, which we round to 8,050. 
▪
For our other models, which use the 11/20/26 close price as a starting point, we think it’s most important to focus on the return signal they are 
sending and the reason behind that signal. Among those, our most optimistic model is our sentiment model (a +15.1% return signal), which 
reflects the idea that investor moods have already turned significantly bearish amid the recent pullback in stocks and are sending a contrarian 
buy signal. Our other models bake in a solid +2% real GDP growth trajectory in the year ahead, only a few more Fed hikes, and the idea that the 
S&P 500’s current forecast earnings yield will come to fruition. These models generate 12-month forward return signals of 10.2%, 13.4% and 
13.7%.  Looking across all of our models, more hikes than expected, slower GDP growth than expected, and corporate earnings growth that 
comes in less robust than expected are the biggest risks we see to a constructive view on the year ahead.

Our LTM P/E Model Points To Further Multiple Compression, Fair Value For The S&P 500 of 8,063
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
We use a new eight-variable model to project where the S&P 500 LTM P/E should be in 4Q27 based on RBCCM macro forecasts. The 
philosophy of the model is that equity valuations are driven by the cost of capital (interest rates and monetary policy, including both levels and 
change), the trajectory of economic growth (both growth rates and acceleration/deceleration), and the momentum of corporate earnings. We then 
take the projected P/E and multiply it against the bottom-up consensus EPS growth forecast to get a fair value estimate for the S&P 500 in 4Q27. 
Note, we’ve also taken a look at the implied P/E using consensus macro forecasts and have run several stress tests on the P/E which take 
inflation and interest rate assumptions higher.  
▪
This model replaces an earlier version that we’ve used in the past. Key differences are time frame (this one starts in the early 1990’s instead of 
the 1960’s, as we’ve moved from an average trailing 4Q S&P 500 P/E to the actual LTM S&P 500 P/E) and inputs (we added rates of change to 
for interest rates in addition to levels, plus acceleration/deceleration in S&P 500 EPS growth as a gauge of EPS sentiment).

RBC Capital Markets
8
Flows – After A Period of Weaker Trends, US Equity Inflows Have Improved
US equity fund flows remain in positive territory, but recently returned to their 2024 and mid 2026 highs, pointing to risk of a peak. After 
retreating meaningfully from the midyear highs in the fall, the trend has been one of improvement in recent updates. The previous 
slowdown was more pronounced among domestic buyers than foreign ones, but both are still allocating to the US in recent updates. 
Meanwhile, bond flows have stayed strong, with only a modest pullback. 
Investor Sentiment & Positioning – Deepening Bearishness & Peaking Bullishness
On the weekly AAII survey, our main gauge which is an input into our price target, net bullishness has been falling but is already down 
to levels that are typically followed by strong 12-month forward returns in the S&P 500. While this indicator is not back to the lows 
associated with deeper periods of challenge in the US equity market like 2022 and 2025, it has gotten close to levels that have often 
marked a low over the last year. Separately, stock market optimists in the Conference Board consumer survey appear to have topped 
out near historical highs. Similarly, margin debt has stalled after hitting new all-time highs. All of this suggests the retail buy the dip 
mentality may fade. On the institutional side, CFTC equity futures positioning data has also started to slip after hitting levels one 
standard deviation above the long-term average. Financial conditions remain in accommodative territory but have eased back as well. 
Overall, a contrarian buy signal is in place on one model longer-term, though it and our other indicators suggest sentiment still has room 
to erode.
Valuations – Our Modeling Points to Moderate Gains in the Year Ahead, Some Important Thresholds Reached
Our main valuation model, which is an input into our price target, points to a fair value estimate for the S&P 500 of around ~8,000 for 
4Q27. It bakes in the bottom-up consensus EPS outlook and a LTM P/E of 18.67x, which represents significant compression from here 
due to the slowing rate of EPS growth that’s currently embedded in consensus expectations in coming quarters. The P/E calculation is 
based on several macro variables and reflects pressures from the challenges of interest rates, inflation, and decelerating EPS growth 
rates. Meanwhile, our earnings yield gap model, another input into our price target, also points to gains in the index over the next 12 
months, but is close to a range that points to negative returns for stocks on a 12-month forward basis. Our bottom-up NTM and FY2 P/E 
indicators for the S&P 500 recently returned to their last three major lows, suggesting to us the US equity market was ready to make a 
stand after the Nov 5th drop, but admittedly these did not get back to the lows associated with 2022 and COVID.  
Earnings – Strong But Possibly Peaking, With Questions & Concerns About The Future Trajectory
2026 and 3Q26 EPS growth have come in well ahead of expectations, with 2026’s strong growth rate propped up by the Tech sector. 
Note, operating margin forecasts are currently pointing to a dip in 2028 relative to 2027 on consensus forecasts. Separately, we had 
been seeing a deceleration in the rate of upward revisions (earnings sentiment) for the S&P 500 in the last reporting season, but this 
stat has stabilized. Two concerns are 1) the potential for Iran war impacts to pressure 2027 forecasts, and 2) whether expectations 
around the AI theme and data centers are too frothy. We may not get answers to either question until the next reporting season starts 
and year-ahead outlooks are issued. 2028 may end up being more relevant than 2027 to the 2nd question, but this will take some time 
to understand.

RBC Capital Markets
9
Our Current Take On The Drivers of the US Equity Market – Fading Positives & Emerging  Concerns
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy
Economic Backdrop – A Solid Foundation, But Not Immune From the Concerns of the Day
Consensus and RBC Economics expect real GDP to be in a favorable range for US equities for the foreseeable future, as the S&P 
500 tends to post gains of ~10% in 12-month periods that see GDP end up in the 2.1-3% range on a year-over-year basis. While 
consumer views around employment remain subdued, labor market indicators are generally behaving well with no signs of the 
deterioration typically seen during major drawdowns. Consumer confidence has been weak but is showing signs of stabilization and 
we are keeping a close eye out for signs of improvement post the mid-terms given historically low views on government policy 
heading in. Improvement here could help spark leadership rotation in US equities. The AI story is a key risk to monitor, given the feed 
through of AI capex to the broader economy. 
Monetary Policy – Hiking Ahead, Additional Hikes Recently Added To The Forecast
After a period of stability, the Fed outlook has seen a renewal of uncertainty recently. Financial market participants have recently 
begun to add more hikes into the forecast, something that tends to create downward pressure on US equities broadly. Our modeling 
suggests that the S&P 500 can post strong gains in 12-month periods which see hikes in the 25-100 basis point range. Our work also 
shows that when a fast/chunky hiking cycle is avoided, stocks tend to incur pain at the onset of hikes (median drawdown of ~10%) 
before rallying back over the duration of the hiking cycle. Fed commentary highlighting the possibility of larger hikes has been a 
significant problem that has emerged in the backdrop for stocks recently, as the damage done to stocks during hiking cycles with an 
average move closer to 50 bps have been more difficult for the stock market to recover from. 
Government Policy – Gridlock Anticipated, With A Less Favorable Balance of Power In Place, AI Headwinds 
The midterm election only seemed to replace one set of uncertainties with another (at worst) or a lack of positives (at best). Most US 
equity investors don’t expect the new Congress to accomplish much in terms of new legislation, but we think it makes sense to keep 
an eye on efforts to address big issues that drove voters to the polls including affordability, corruption (where corporate greed and the 
Tech/Health Care sectors were called out), Health Care, the Iran war, and data center/AI regulation. If nothing else, these could lay 
the groundwork for new legislation in 2029 and beyond. Separately, government spending is expected to be lackluster again in coming 
years after a period of strength. Note, the S&P 500 tends to rise 5% in years with a Republican President and Democratic Congress, 
the least rewarding balance of power backdrop over time. State level AI regulation impulses are an issue we are monitoring. Note, we 
think the debate over AI regulation and the fate of the capex cycle is a conversation that will be with us throughout 2027. 
Deals & Cash Deployment – Strong Trends, With Capex Concerns In the Spotlight
S&P 500 capex growth has been hitting new all-time highs, but US equity investors have become increasingly fearful that a peak is in 
place over the past several months. The role that AI played in the midterm results has added fuel to that fire, though most investors 
and analysts we speak with are in the camp the data center spend will remain strong, perhaps slowing down a bit (elongating the 
cycle), changing geographies, and becoming more expensive (to work around community concerns), but that the cycle is not over. 
We’d add that spending to address safety concerns (i.e. cyber risk) and infrastructure (to allow the technology to work) is something 
we suspect will ramp up. Going unnoticed – buyback and capex trends remain solid and debt levels are not worrisome despite the rise 
in rates. Investors are also contemplating whether M&A might pick up ahead of the 2028 Presidential election, which carries the risk of 
ushering in a less favorable regulatory backdrop for deals.

RBC Capital Markets
10
The Four Tiers of Fear in US Equity Markets & Where We Are Now
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy
▪
This is a framework we’ve developed to think about potential downside levels in the S&P 500 whenever a period of stress and uncertainty 
emerges. It is generally based on our own lived professional experience in the US equity market since 2000.
▪
As of the 11/20/26 close, the S&P 500 had moved beyond tier 1 territory, with the S&P 500 down 11.6% from its most recent all-time high.  
While it’s possible that a bottom has been put in already (the 1997 and 1999 pullbacks bottomed out at a similar level), it would not 
surprise us if the pullback goes a little deeper into tier 2 territory, as there has tended to be an air pocket between 10% and 14% in the 
post GFC era. We aren’t in tier 3 camp, as we think the risk of economic and/or EPS contraction remains relatively low. 
▪
Even after the current period of distress abates, we remain on guard for additional choppiness in stocks in early 2027. Cyber events are a 
new threat by historical standards, which we could see easily turning into a tier 2 drawdown if a significant event or set of events does 
occur. Other potential potholes includes downward revisions to 2027 and/or 2028 forecasts, though the latter may not occur until later in 
the year. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Drawdown Framework
Rules of Thumb
Equivalent S&P 500 
Downside Levels To 
Keep In Mind Today vs. 
The 10/6/2026 High
Additional Thoughts
Tier 1
Garden-Variety Pullback
▪5–10% 
drawdown
▪7,037–7,428
▪The natural starting point for thinking about downside risk. 
Tier 2
Growth Scare
▪14–20% 
drawdown
▪6,255–6,724
▪The US equity market has experienced five of these since the GFC: in 2010 (European debt crisis), 2011 (US 
debt downgrade), 2015-2016 (industrial recession), 2018 (trade war / Fed fears over balance sheet unwind), 
and early 2025 (tariff tantrum).
▪These were all periods in which equity market participants began to significantly price in a crisis or recession 
that ultimately did not materialize. Most of these also saw at least one quarter of weak real GDP growth. 
▪Since the GFC, we’ve not had any pullbacks between the 10% and 14% range. Looking back to the 1950s, 
we had a few that fell in between in the 11-12% area – 1997 (Asian Financial Crisis) and 1999 (inflation/oil 
prices, Fed hikes, and Y2K anxiety). 
Tier 3
Recession or Major Shock
▪Median & 
average 
drawdown of 
27% and 32%
▪5,317–5,708
▪Recession drawdowns have ranged from 14% to 57% since the late 1930s.
▪Recent, major wars have been similar to recessions, with the drawdowns around 9/11 and the two Gulf Wars 
ranging from 20% to 34%. 
▪2022 has been a tough year to characterize, with the 25% drop driven by the Russia-Ukraine war and an 
interest rate shock. Recession fears were also high and the Misery Index was at levels consistent with 
recession. One stat that helps explain this drawdown: since the mid-1950s, drawdowns worse than 10% from 
the all-time high have had a median drop of 22% and an average drop of 26%. 
Tier 4
Major Crisis
▪Around 50% or 
more
▪3,909
▪49% drawdown for the Tech bubble and 57% for the Great Financial Crisis
▪Looking farther back in time, 43% drawdown for WW2 vs. the high at the start of the war (more than 80% vs. 
the pre-Great Depression high). Additionally, in the Nixon era the index fell 48% from Jan 1973 to October 
1974 in a tumultuous period including Watergate, rising rates, the Nifty 50 collapse and the Arab oil embargo. 

RBC Capital Markets
11
The S&P 500 Has Fallen Out of Tier 1 Territory, and Is Now On The Cusp of Tier 2 Drawdown Territory
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy; Bloomberg
▪
As of the November 20th close, the S&P 500 was down 11.6% from its most recent all-time high, in between what we’d consider to be a 
tier 1 garden-variety pullback and a tier 2 growth scare.  Since the GFC, we’ve had five tier 2 growth scares (down 14-20% vs. the post 
GFC or all-time high), which all involved a recession near-miss or the emergence of an unknown shock that investors worried could cause 
conditions to spiral out of control and take us there or to another crisis. One of these occurred near the midterm elections (2018), though 
it’s fair to say that was a contributor to the drawdown, not the main catalyst for it. In 2018, Fed policy was also a key contributor. 
▪
Post GFC, we’ve also had two tier 3 drawdowns (around a quarter to a third of the market’s value lost) – the COVID recession drop of 
34% and the 25% decline around the interest rate shock and Russia-Ukraine war of 2022 (also a midterm year). 
▪
There have also been plenty of tier 1 (5-10%) drawdowns including the 7.4% drawdown ahead of the 2014 midterm election and the 9.1% 
drop earlier this year after the Iran war was started. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Drawdown Framework

An Overview Of Past, Major Drawdowns (11% or More) In The S&P 500 vs. The All-Time High
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy; Bloomberg
▪
Using Bloomberg’s drawdown function, we pulled together a list of all of the S&P 500 drawdowns (measured from the index’s most recent 
all-time high at the time, to a clear trough). Which amounted to roughly 11% or more, starting in 1956. This allowed us to isolate a list of 
major drawdowns that were worse than the tier 1 / garden variety pullbacks of 5-10% in our four tiers of fear framework, that we consider 
to be fairly normal, though admittedly unpleasant in the moment. 
▪
We’ve listed major trends in economic data, geopolitical events, and other stock market relevant events that occurred during or near the 
drawdown. Stretched valuations, energy shocks, rising rates, financial market breakages, unusual events, and prolonged periods of angst 
were all recurring features. In terms of macro indicators, we also tend to see earnings and GDP declines, weakness in labor market and 
capex indicators, and sharp drops in sentiment. 
▪
Drawdowns like the one we had in place as of the November 20th, 2026, close (11.6%) have been rare since the mid 1950’s but have 
occurred a few times. Both were in the late 1990’s around the Asian Financial Crisis (1997, 10.8%) and in the midst of another period of 
angst around inflation (following a rebound in oil prices), Fed hikes (a new cycle began in June with another hike in August), and unusual 
events (Y2K anxiety).  
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Drawdown Framework

After a Period of Trending Lower, US Equity Flows Have Improved
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy; EPFR
▪
US equity flows had been trending below their summer highs this fall, but recently have picked up. 
▪
Meanwhile, US bond flows are modestly off their summer highs, but have generally held up in recent months. 
▪
Overall, we have been seeing a stronger flow tailwind for bonds than equities within the US, though it’s worth noting that four-week 
average bond flows are pointing to some slowing. Note, flows to US equities have returned to late 2024 and mid 2026 highs in the latest 
updates. Considering how US equities have traded recently, we wouldn’t be surprised to see the flows take a hit in the following data 
updates.

Our Main Sentiment Model Now Points To A Gain Of 15.1% Over The Next 12 Months
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, AAII, S&P
▪
As of 11/19/26, net bulls were at -8.90% on the weekly update (an improvement vs. the prior week’s -20.60%) and -11.85% on the four-
week average (in line with the prior week). 
▪
The four-week average is still between one standard deviation and two standard deviations below the long-term average, a level that has, 
on average, been followed by a gain of 15.1% in the S&P 500 on a 12-month-forward basis. 
▪
As shown in the lower right chart, the recent high on the weekly unadjusted data point was in line with levels that have marked the last few 
peaks. The previous week’s reading was in line with most of the post 2025 tariff lows, now including the Iran war low, but the 2025 tariff 
low was still far off. Overall, what we’re seeing on this indicator is evidence of deeply bearish sentiment, but not the kind of extreme lows 
associated with more significant crises and periods of intense challenge.

Stock Market Optimism Among Consumers Remains High, But Has Stalled
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg. Conference Board data through October 2026. 
▪
This is a gauge of retail investor enthusiasm. 
▪
The most recent high in stock market optimism occurred after Trump’s win in the 2024 Presidential election. It dropped sharply during 
tariffs, then rebounded quickly, and has remained elevated but off that late 2024 high ever since. 
▪
Consumer bears on the stock market have also been hovering near historical lows.  
▪
Overall, we see this as a signal that investors need to be on guard for a waning of willingness among retail investors to buy the dip.

Margin Debt Has Stalled, Coinciding With The Recent Period of Chop in the S&P 500
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, FINRA, Bloomberg
▪
Margin debt serves as a proxy for investor risk appetite and speculative activity, signaling that investors are increasingly confident (or 
complacent),and willing to use leverage (or not) to amplify returns. Sharp declines often coincide with — or accelerate — market selloffs.
▪
In the latest updates, margin debt has moved sideways and is slightly below its August peak. 
▪
This has coincided with the recent stalling out in the S&P 500 and may signal that the retail investor can no longer be counted on to buy 
the dips.

S&P 500 Outlook / Investor Sentiment & Positioning

S&P 500 Forward P/Es Recently Returned To Levels That Marked The Last Few Important Lows
▪On November 19th, the NTM P/E recently returned to ~21.0x, around the lows of the Iran war and the 2025 tariffs and almost back to the October 2023

in place after the November 5th sell-off. All of that being said, a more significant downturn like 2022 and COVID when the long-term average was

RBC Capital Markets
22
Bottom-Up, Mkt Cap Wtd S&P 500 NTM P/E Compression Has Already Been Significant
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, S&P Capital IQ/Clarifi
▪
Using the high in place since the start of the year, the market cap weighted S&P 500 NTM P/E recently got back down to levels in line with 
their early 2025 tariff lows. The amount of compression has also exceeded what has been seen around the typical major drawdown in the 
S&P 500 index over time. 
▪
Note major drawdowns are defined as a drop of significantly more than 11% in the S&P 500 from peak to trough based on daily close 
prices, in which the peak is defined as the most recent all-time high. 
▪
We haven’t seen the same degree of compression in the equal weighted NTM P/E as we have in the market cap weighted NTM P/E in 
early 2026.  On an equal weighted basis, the compression isn’t quite back to the median move we’ve seen in past major drawdowns. 
However, it is in line with the compression seen in late 2024/early 2025 and the 2015-2016 drawdown. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Valuations

RBC Capital Markets
23
Our FY2 EPS Yield Analysis Points To Low Teens Gains For The S&P 500 In The Next 12 Months
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy
▪
The gap between the next 12 months’ earnings yields in the S&P 500 and the 10-year Treasury yield has fallen to roughly -0.02%, putting 
in a range that has historically been followed by a +13.4% gain in the S&P 500 over the next 12 months.
▪
Though the model is still in constructive territory for now, it’s worth noting that it is approaching the danger zone.  A move into the -1% to    
-2% range on this indicator has tended to be associated with negative 12-month forward returns in the S&P 500 of -3.2%. Holding the 
S&P 500’s FY2 current earnings yield constant at 5.02%, a move in the 10-year Treasury yield above 6.02% would take this indicator into 
that range.

RBC Capital Markets
24
Tracking The Bottom-Up Consensus For S&P 500 EPS
▪In our valuation and price target modeling, we are utilizing the bottom-up consensus S&P 500 EPS forecast as tracked by Bloomberg. 
▪For 4Q26, the trailing dollar value per share level is $356 (which also currently reflects a full-year, calendar-year 2026 level). This has improved significantly 
since the start of the year where it started out at $313. The current bottom-up consensus forecast bakes in some operating margin expansion for the broader 
index this year and revenue growth of 14%. The anticipated yr/yr growth in EPS for 4Q26, on a trailing four-quarter basis, is 28%, up from the 13% growth 
rate for 4Q25. 
▪For 4Q27, the growth rate is expected to slow to 20%. That EPS dollar value is currently at $429. 
Key Takeaways

Note: Operating margin stats exclude REITs and Financials; this data is maintained by Bloomberg and can be found on the BI STOX page on Bloomberg – an interactive model is not available from the 
RBC US Equity Strategy team.

RBC Capital Markets
28
The Return Of Downward Revisions Could Further Compress S&P 500 NTM P/E Multiples
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, S&P Capital IQ/Clarifi
▪
In the post-COVID era, we’ve seen a clear link between the rate of upward EPS estimate revisions in the S&P 500 and the forward P/E 
multiple of the index. As the rate of upward revisions rises, the P/E tends to rise as well. As the rate of upward EPS estimate revisions 
falls, the P/E tends to come down. 
▪
We admittedly didn’t see this relationship throughout much of the post GFC/pre-COVID era, at least until late in the decade. This 
relationship did appear to be loosely in place in the pre- and post-Tech bubble period. 
Key Takeaways

RBC Capital Markets
29
When S&P 500 EPS Forecasts Start Out Too High, They Tend To Be Off by 8-12%
▪S&P 500 EPS estimates normally start out the year too high, but stabilize by mid-year. On an average basis, most cuts to S&P 500 EPS forecasts are 
fully implemented by 2Q of the year in question as forecasts don’t tend to change much in the 2nd half. 
▪The median cut in downward adjustment years has been 8%, while the average cut has been 12%. 
▪The median increase in upward adjustment years has been 12%, while the average increase has been 10%. 
Key Takeaways

30
Looking Back At 2018-2019 And 2022-2023 EPS Growth Forecasts
▪Around the first Trump tariffs, S&P 500 EPS forecasts (bottom-up, consensus) were resilient for 2018, but steadily fell for 2019. 
▪A similar dynamic was seen around the Russia-Ukraine war. 2022 EPS forecasts fell a little in the second half of the year, but took a bigger hit for 2023 
early on. 
▪This has contributed to our concern that earnings risks from the Middle East conflict may lie more in 2027 than 2026.

32
Significant Problems On The Earnings Front Tend To Be Seen During Major Drawdowns
S&P 500 Outlook / Earnings
▪Major drawdowns (11% or more, peak to trough) have often been associated with outright declines in corporate profits on a year-over-year basis. 
▪In recent decades, we’ve also tended to see the rate of upward EPS estimate revisions for the S&P 500 turn deeply negative.
▪Forward P/Es for the S&P 500 also tend to compress, but trailing P/E trends have been more mixed over time.

Expectations For The Real GDP Backdrop Have Been Solid From A Stock Market Perspective
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
RBC Economics is anticipating 2.3% yr/yr for 4Q27, while the consensus is tracking at 2.2% yr/yr per Bloomberg. 
▪
As a result, in our December price target update, we are baking in the average return for the S&P 500 in the 2.1-3% range of 10.2%. 
▪
Some investors are concerned a slowdown in AI capex could send the US economy into a slower growth environment, but we are not 
currently seeing downgrades. To the contrary, full year consensus estimates for 2026-2028 have been inching up recently.  If we were to 
see real GDP forecasts slip down into the 1.1-2% range, our model’s S&P 500 return signal for the next 12 months would fall to 6.3%.
Key Takeaways

Consumer Confidence / Sentiment Has Been Weak But Hasn’t Hit New Lows In The Latest Updates
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg. UMich data as of November 13, 2026; Conference Board data as of October 27, 2026. 
▪
Conference Board consumer confidence continued to show signs of attempted stabilization if one zooms out and looks at trends over the 
past few months. The COVID lows are essentially holding on that survey. The latest October data showed a jump in confidence rising 
from 84.2 to 91.7, coming in better than expected. 
▪
On the U Mich consumer sentiment survey, the November data improved following October’s decline, with headline sentiment ticking up 
from 48.0 to 51.5. The low from May is still in place. 
▪
Despite the poor headlines and disappointment, we continue to think these charts paint a picture of consumer resilience amid intense 
pressures.

Signs Of Damage To The Broader Economy Tend To Emerge During Or Around Major Drawdowns
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver, Bloomberg, S&P 
▪
Declining ISM manufacturing and new orders tend to be seen during major drawdowns, including those in the post-GFC environment. 
▪
This has also been the case for industrial production, though it’s worth noting that the post-COVID drawdowns have been an exception. 
▪
Real GDP growth (q/q) also tends to take some hits (negative quarters or severe slowing) during or around major drawdowns, and this 
has also generally been true post COVID and post GFC. 
▪
Note, trends on all of these indicators are still constructive for now, other than ISM manufacturing which has stalled slightly. Consensus 
forecasts for real GDP q/q are anticipating growth in the 2.1-2.2% range throughout 2027.

The First Year in the S&P 500 Under A New Fed Chair
Bernanke (2/1/06)
Yellen (2/3/14)
Powell (2/5/18)
Warsh (5/22/26)
The Stock Market Tends To Stumble When A New Era Of Leadership At The Fed Begins 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
The S&P 500 traded sideways after Warsh took over as Chair of the Federal Reserve in late May through early August, before 
rebounding.
▪
It’s worth noting that the stock market also traded in a very choppy fashion after Bernanke and Powell stepped into the role. 
▪
The stock market moved up as Yellen took over but soon traded sideways for a period of time (March–April). 
▪
Ultimately, Warsh’s three predecessors all saw the stock market end up higher a year after they took over. During their first years, 
Bernanke and Powell both did experience a phase in which the stock market traded below the level it was at on their first day. In the case 
of Powell, a growth scare (20% drop) occurred, which was driven in part by concerns about Fed policy error. Yellen was the only one of 
the three that managed to escape this fate, though she did have several periods of choppiness during her first year. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Monetary Policy

Increased Expectations For Hikes In The Year Ahead Contributed To The Recent Drop In Stocks
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy; Bloomberg, S&P
▪
Earlier this fall, we saw a return and ramping up of Fed rate hike expectations in the year ahead, which was a headwind for the US equity

▪

for stocks had been lessening.  That respite proved short-lived, however. 
▪
In recent updates, hikes have been getting added back renewing this pressure on the US equity market. Some of this was pulled out after 
the big drop in the S&P 500 on November 20th.

How We Think About Fed Moves In The Context Of Longer-Term US Equity Market Moves
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P
▪
Historically, when the Fed has made modest cuts in a 12-month period (that amount to 1% or less), the S&P 500 has gone up by 13.3% 
on average. When the Fed hikes modestly (in the 1% or less range), returns tend to be similar (13.7%). Deeper cuts and bigger hikes 
during a 12-month window tend to be accompanied by weaker equity market returns or declines. RBC US Rates Strategy is forecasting 
the Fed to hike two more times over the next 12 months.  In our latest price target update, we have baked in the idea of 2 additional hikes 
from here and a 13.7% forward return through YE 2027. If the Fed forecast were to move into the 1.1-2% range (for more than 100 and up 
to 200 basis points of hikes), the average return signal would fall to 7.4%. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Monetary Policy 
Price Target Model

RBC Capital Markets
45
The S&P 500 Incurs More Lasting Damage From Chunky Hiking Cycles
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver, Bloomberg, S&P 
▪
For each hiking cycle, a meaningful drawdown in the S&P 500 was seen right around liftoff. Peaks in the S&P 500 tended to occur before 
the first hike, but a couple came right as hikes began or shortly after. Ex 2022, which ran much deeper, these drawdowns ranged from -
8.2% to -14.2%, with the median coming in at -9.6%. Meaningful lows tended to come 1-3.5 months after the first hike.  
▪
1994-1995 and 2022 were much more aggressive hiking cycles than the ones that occurred in 1997, 1999-2000, 2004-2006, and 2015-
2016 For 94-95 and 2022, the average move was more than 40 basis points. In the other hiking cycles, the median move was in the 25-29 
bps range. 1994-1995 and 2022 saw the market dig down into a hole in the first half of the hiking cycle then spend the second half digging 
its way out. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Monetary Policy

RBC Capital Markets
46
In More Gradual Cycles With Smaller Hikes, The S&P 500 Tends To Stumble Initially Then Recover 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver, Bloomberg, S&P 
▪
Each of these hiking cycles saw a drop around lift off. As noted on the previous page, the average size of each hike was modest in the 25-
29 bps range. 
▪
After the initial drops, the stock market would go on to rise through the end of each hiking cycle. Admittedly, major peaks were achieved 
toward the tail end of the 1999-2000 hiking cycle and shortly after the 2004-2006 hiking cycle. In the case of the 2000 peak, other issues 
contributed to the severe decline in stocks including the 9/11 terrorist attack, accounting scandals/major investor frauds, and the Iraq War. 
In both instances, we think it also simply took time for the impact of higher interest rates to filter into financial markets. 
Key Takeaways

Average S&P 500 Returns When Different Political Parties Are In Control Of Government
Under A Republican President & Democratic Congress, S&P 500 Returns Tend To Be Positive But Low 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver, Includes years since 1932; the 2000 election resulted in a 50/50 tie in the Senate (with a Republican VP acting as the deciding vote after inauguration). However, 
midway through 2001 one Republican Senator left to become an Independent and caucus with the Democrats; updated through 2025. 
▪
The stock market tends to rise in all political backdrops. 
▪
Under a Republican President, annual returns tend to be just 5% when Democrats control both chambers of Congress.
▪
Returns tend to be strongest under united Republican control or a Democratic President/Split or Republican Congress.

Average Return When Neither Q3 or Q4 Is Down
Stocks Tend To Be Strong Following Midterms, But The Set-up Matters
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy
▪
When the stock market falls in either 3Q or 4Q of the midterm year, the average return in the following calendar year tends to be 16.9%. 
▪
But when the stock market doesn’t fall in either of those quarters during the midterm year, the average return in the following calendar 
year drops to just 6.2%. 
▪
In 2026, 3Q was up 2%. 4Q is down 6.2% as of mid November. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Government Policy

Consumer Confidence Rises After Midterm Election Years About Half Of The Time
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Haver
▪
When confidence is extremely depressed going into the election, robust recoveries tend to be seen. However, the current reading is close 
to the long-term average, suggesting there is no strong signal at the moment. This reflects the idea that while consumer confidence has 
come down, there is some inherent resiliency in place. 
▪
Improvements in confidence were seen around the wave elections of the past few decades, though these were often followed by 
choppiness or ended up being short-lived. This includes the 2014 red wave, which had a similar level of confidence heading in as 2026. 
1994 is another point of comparison we often hear from the political community. That year saw confidence pop initially then turn choppy 
and come down a little. 
Key Takeaways
S&P 500 Outlook / Government Policy

62
The AI Buildout Has Accelerated, Providing a Meaningful Boost to US Economic Growth 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, U.S. Census Bureau, Bureau of Economic Analysis
▪
According to the Federal Reserve’s July 2026 FEDS Note, “The AI Buildout and the Economy: Publicly Available Data to Assess AI’s 
Impact,” the AI infrastructure buildout has continued to accelerate, with private data center construction spending approaching $60 billion, 
up from roughly $10 billion in 2021.
▪
The Fed’s analysis also suggests that AI-related investment has provided meaningful support to US GDP growth, particularly through 
software and computing equipment spending, although rising equipment imports have partially offset the contribution.

63
AI Adoption Is Rising, With Highly Exposed Industries Seeing Stronger Productivity Gains
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, U.S. Census Bureau, Business Trends and Outlook Survey, Bureau of Labor Statistics, Bureau of Economic Analysis.
Note: Regarding the right panel: High AI exposure industries include Information, Finance, Professional and Business Services; Medium AI exposure industries include Wholesale Trade, Manufacturing, 
Education, and Health Services; Low AI exposure industries include Utilities, Construction, Leisure * Hospitality, Transportation, and Warehousing.
▪
The July 2026 Fed study also highlights rising AI adoption across US businesses, particularly among larger firms, with adoption rates 
approaching 40% for businesses with 250+ employees versus roughly 20% for smaller firms.
▪
Broader productivity gains remain less evident, however, as the Fed finds that while AI exposed industries have generally experienced 
stronger productivity growth, overall productivity trends have remained relatively stable over time.

RBC Capital Markets
64
S&P 500 Capex Growth Has Hit a New High, Amid Worries About a Peak In The Cycle
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
Preliminary data for 3Q26 has showed continued strength and a major new high for the broader market. 
▪
We have seen some meaningful recovery in recent quarters outside of the top-10 names as well. While the top-10 cohort is close to past 
highs, we believe there is still ample room for capex growth to return to past peaks in the rest of the index. 
▪
The elevated rate of capex growth we’ve been seeing in the S&P 500 and its top-10 market cap names puts some context around current 
AI jitters, as this is a natural point to worry about whether the capex story may be peaking. We think catch-up from names outside the top 
10 could help elongate the cycle. 
▪
Note, ISM new orders is a key variable to watch, as it tends to accompany increases in capex.

RBC Capital Markets
65
Ramp-Up In IPO Activity Tends To Be Good For Stocks 
▪
Some investors have been spooked by the surge in IPO activity in dollar value terms. Admittedly, peaks in IPO activity in dollar value 
terms have coincided with peaks in the S&P 500 in the past. We would argue that it was the decline in peak, not the surge, that was the 
problem, as it reflected a ratcheting down in risk appetite.  
▪
2026 dynamics are less concerning when viewed through the lens of deal counts. From that perspective, the IPO cycle is still very early 
days. What we see from that angle as well is that high levels of activity can endure for quite some time, which coincides with sustained 
moves higher in equity prices. 
Key Takeaways
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy , Bloomberg, S&P

71
Effective Tax Rates Are Creeping Up
▪
The effective tax rate S&P 500 companies pay moved sharply lower during Trump’s first term due to tax reform and the dramatic cut in the 
headline rate that was enacted. 
▪
In recent years, it has drifted up. We’ve not seen much of a change so far in 2026. 
▪
The median company’s rate is higher than the broader index’s on a market cap weighted basis.

US vs. Non -US Developed Markets
2

RBC Capital Markets
78
Our Current Take On The Drivers of US vs. Non-US – No Clear Edge
Flows –  US Equity Flows Have Improved, While Global & Globel Ex US Flows Have Been Resilient
After a brief period of deterioration, US equity flows are improving again. Meanwhile, flows to global equity funds and global ex US equity funds have been 
resilient. Generally, we’ve also seen strength/improvement in North America (Canada) and Asia (i.e. Japan) along with Emerging Markets, though there 
was a little weakness in Japan/Far East in our latest update. The improvement that had been seen in Western Europe in mid 2026 has faded. 
Investor Sentiment & Positioning – Slipping Across The Globe
The Sentix indices point to a stall in sentiment around the globe, with weak conditions for Europe. The US has been weak relative to history for quite some 
time, however. 
Valuations – US Equities Near Post COVID Lows Relative to Non-US Developed Markets Equities
After returning to its five-year average (which has been marking a top in US and US growth leadership in 2026) in late September / early October, the 
US/non-US developed markets P/E is approaching but not quite back to post COVID lows. The US is also looking undervalued versus both Europe and 
Far East on this metric. The US is also back down to 20-year averages vs. the major DM regions and countries we’re tracking. Meanwhile, versus their 
own history, forward P/Es for most of the global developed markets we track have been easing but are still a little above average. 
Earnings – Earnings Sentiment Attempting To Recapture Dominance For The US
We have been seeing a deterioration in earnings sentiment across global developed markets. The pain was more acute in the US than most other areas, 
but we have seen a bit of a recovery in the US while other regions/countries have remained weak in our latest update. 
US 
Favors
S&P 500 Outlook / Overview
Neutral 
Neutral 
Non-US
Neutral 
Neutral 
Economic Backdrop – The US Has Been Shining Brightest From A GDP Perspective, But Is Expected To Fade Slightly In Coming Years
Real GDP forecasts are stronger for the US than the other developed markets economies we’re tracking for 2026 - 2028, and US forecasts have been 
inching up. Non-US 2027 forecasts have been flat or lower. We do note, however, that annual growths are expected to be a little lower in 2027-2028 than 
2026 for the US, but the opposite is true for Canada and Western Europe. US consumer confidence appears to be stabilizing (admittedly at very weak 
levels), while Europe and Australia remain in a downtrend. US and European business cycle indicators are picking up. On inflation, US CPI expectations 
are the highest among economies we’re tracking. US strength is admittedly coming under scrutiny from concerns about the AI capex narrative, but we are 
not yet seeing any deterioration in the data that speaks to this occurring. 
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates – Central Banks Are Tightening Around the Globe
The US has joined other central banks in starting to tighten. 
Sector Differentials – Political Headwinds For AI and the Growth Trade Are A Headwind For the US
US/non-US performance has been moving in sync with US Growth/Value performance – no surprise, since the US equity market remains much more 
exposed to AI through the Technology and Communication Services sectors, while non-US developed equity markets are much more exposed to the 
cyclically value oriented sectors like Financials and Industrials. To the extent the US midterms ushered in a new period of scrutiny for AI and the likelihood 
of greater regulation (potentially slowing the capex cycle), the US would be more adversely impacted.   
Neutral 
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy

79
Major Inflections in US/Non-US DM Relative Performance Are Occurring Alongside Important US Events
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, MSCI; please see the MSCI disclaimer at the end of this report; regions/countries based on MSCI constituents with negative earners excluded
▪
In 2026, the US relative performance versus non-US peaked alongside the US Tech trade heading into the midterm election, then 
attempted to put in a bottom after the broader US equity market and Tech sector attempted to put in a bottom on November 5th, shortly 
after the election. 
▪
Earlier this year, the US started to outperform again after the Iran war began, ending a brief period of geographical rotation into non-US. 
That trade lasted until early June before the US lost some momentum.

RBC Capital Markets
82
US Equity Inflows Have Improved, Europe’s Improvement Has Stalled, Asia Picking Up Until Recently
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, EPFR
▪
After a period of weaker US inflows over the past few months, the most recent weekly updates have seen an uptick in US equity flows.
▪
Western European funds had been seeing some improvement, but that has stalled in the latest updates. There clearly was a weakening of 
trend in the US and improvement for Europe earlier this year, but we were not seeing an outright rotation from US to Europe. The last 
data updates have given us more confidence that US has remained favored over Europe, but the most recent weekly flows to Europe 
have been positive too.
▪
Flows to Asia-Pacific region are seeing a stronger trend lately, but may be peaking now. Latam flows remain highly volatile, with strong 
inflows turning into sharp outflows recently.

US Equity Market Has Been Dominated By Growth / AI Driven Sectors
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, MSCI; please see the MSCI disclaimer at the end of this report; regions/countries based on MSCI constituents with negative earners excluded

Growth vs. Value
3

RBC Capital Markets
104
Our Current Take On The Drivers of Growth vs. Value – Edge To Value
Flows –  Mostly Weak for Both Styles
Both Growth and Value are seeing outflows. At the margin, we note that Growth outflows are getting less negative, but there have been 
some modest inflows to Value. Overall, we don’t see a strong signal here. 
Investor Sentiment & Positioning – Growth Has Been Crowded
Nasdaq 100 futures data from CFTC points to persistent crowding in the big cap Growth trade. Otherwise, the main thing to keep in mind 
with this trade is that in recent years, Growth has tended to get hit harder when volatility spikes and financial conditions deteriorate. 
Valuations – Nasdaq 100 Valuations Are At A Crossroads, R1000 Growth Valuations Back Down To Average
Nasdaq 100 valuations recently returned to their Iran war and early 2025 tariff lows but remained above their 2022 lows suggesting that a 
shock has been priced in but not a major crisis. Interestingly, Russell 1000 Growth forward P/Es have fallen back down to their long-term 
averages, but Russell 1000 Value forward P/Es remain quite elevated.  
Earnings – Big Cap Growth/AI Trade Losing Dominance
Consensus forecasts continue to embed faster growth rates in AI stocks and the Mag 7 than the rest of the S&P 500 in the years ahead, 
but their dominance is fading as the rate of growth slows for both sides. We are seeing a similar loss of dominance for the Russell 1000 
Growth index vs. Russell 1000 Value on the rate of upward EPS estimate revisions, our favorite gauge of earnings sentiment.  
Value
Neutral 
Growth
Favors
S&P 500 Outlook / Overview
Economic Backdrop – Economic Resilience Favors Value
Value tends to outperform when GDP forecasts are rising. 
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates – No Clear Signal
We’ve not seen a consistent trend for Growth/Value performance during hiking cycles, but have noted that Growth has been lagging when 
inflation is rising. 
Government Policy – Growth Trade Has Headwinds
While Congress is likely to stay gridlocked, we do see broad political headwinds that are blowing against the AI trade and by extension 
the big cap growth trade due to calls for greater data center regulation which have added to concerns about the trajectory of the capex 
cycle. If Congress is able to find common ground on affordability initiatives, that seems more likely to benefit the cyclical Value trade.  
Efforts to address voter affordability concerns could provide some tailwinds for cyclicals, but it is unclear what, if anything, the new 
Congress can accomplish in terms of legislation. 
Deals & Cash Deployment – No Clear Bias
We don’t see major differences in our work between Growth and Value but do note interest expense as a percent of sales has been a bit 
above average in Growth – something we are not seeing for Value.

R1000 Growth Relative to Value
Big Cap Growth Has Been the Trade Off Major Bottoms Over The Past Few Years
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
The most recent bottom in the Growth trade (relative to Value) came after the sharp sell-off that occurred a few days after the midterm 
election. 
▪
The prior peak was in early October. The trough that preceded it came in late July after another positioning in unwind that had occurred 
earlier in the summer. 
▪
Other important inflections in this trade came at the end of March (the Iran war broader market low) and early April 2025 (amid the 
Liberation day tariffs). 
▪
Overall, big cap Growth has lagged this year, with several attempts to fight back. 
Key Takeaways
US Growth vs. Value / Performance

112
Nasdaq 100 Forward P/Es Recently Returned To Levels That Marked The Last Few Important Lows
▪The NTM P/E recently returned to ~23.3x, slightly below the long-term average, which also happened around the Iran war and the 2025 tariffs. The 2022 
low was a bit deeper, however, at 20.4x. Meanwhile, the FY2 P/E hit 20.6x, in line with the last few lows of 21.4x and 20.4x (tariffs, Iran war). These 
were also slightly below the long-term average. 2022 lows were 19.2x, however.

RBC Capital Markets
116
Consensus Still Expects AI Basket & Mag 7 To See Superior Net Income Growth in 2027-2028
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
S&P 500 AI Basket includes tickers: ACN, ADBE, AMD, GOOGL, AMZN, ANET, AVGO, DDOG, INTU, META, MU, MSFT, NVDA, ORCL, PLTR, QCOM, NOW, SMCI. 
▪
S&P 500 net income is expected by consensus to peak in 2026, then decelerate in 2027 and 2028. The Mag 7 appears to have peaked in 
2024, with a brief period of anticipated reacceleration followed by anticipated deceleration through 2028. A broader AI basket is now 
expected to see a peak growth rate in 2026 (a new development in 2Q26 reporting season), with deceleration anticipated in 2027-2028. 
▪
The AI basket and Mag 7 basket are both expected to be stronger than the ex-AI and ex-Mag 7 basket in 2027-2028. Interestingly, the 
Mag 7 bucket is expecting to slightly lag non-Mag 7 for 2026, something that just recently emerged in the data. It is not clear to us if that’s 
a shift or noise in the data. Regardless, until the non-AI and non-Mag 7 baskets start to display superior earnings growth expectations on 
a sustained basis, we suspect the tug of war between mega cap Growth and AI and the rest of the market will persist. We also think the 
resumption of deceleration for the non-Mag 7 and non-AI cohorts in 2027-2028 is a knock against the broadening trade. 
Key Takeaways
US Growth vs. Value / Earnings

RBC Capital Markets
119
EPS Revisions For The S&P 500’s Top-10 Market Cap Names Tracking Weaker Than Rest of Index
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, S&P Capital IQ/ClariFi, CIQ estimates, S&P. For REITs, FFO/share revisions are used instead of EPS revisions
▪
At 90% for the top-10 names recently, this indicator was close to past highs. It then fell sharply. In the latest update, it ticked up to 58.9% 
from the prior 54.4% reading.
▪
We’ve seen a similar pattern in the rest of the index. But there’s been a stronger recovery in the rest of the index in the latest update. 
Note, the ex top-10 basket rose to 64.4% from 63.2%. 
▪
In recent updates, the dominance of the rest of the index has started to emerge, undoing the prior dominance of the top-10 bucket.

▪
Tech and Comm Services have been the two primary laggards, while defensives dominate the list of top performers. Note, Utilities and

A Rundown on Our S&P 500 Sector Recommendations
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg
▪
To arrive at our S&P 500 sector views, we take into account trends in earnings revisions, valuations, flows, our analyst survey work, and 
other qualitative issues specific to different sectors. 
▪
Generally, we are avoiding expensive sectors, and are see more opportunity in Value, Cyclicals, and Defense. 
▪
Our most recent changes were an upgrade to Health Care and downgrades of Technology and Utilities on October 14th.

An All Around Neutral Profile For Communication Services
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Has lagged since early October, but trends have improved in early November, leaving it the second weakest sector overall. 
▪
Upward EPS revisions trends have improved recently and now sit in positive territory.  Valuations are attractive but are veering towards 
neutral on an absolute basis with more appeal on a relative basis. Flows to the Telecom sector as tracked by EPFR (the closest 
comparison for this sector) have stalled in recent updates. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, the US sector came in mixed across the questions we asked, with positive scores on valuation and 
demand, a neutral outlook on the US domestic policy backdrop, but a negative score on performance. 
▪
Note, many non-US investors we speak with are concerned about negative cash flows emerging in the broader “TIMT” space. These cash 
flow concerns are generally sitting in the big Internet names in the Comm Services sector, not Info Tech. On macro indicators, the sector 
is one of the most inversely correlated to gas/diesel prices, outperforming when prices fall.  Rising interest rates are a problem for this 
sector on an equal weighted basis.

RBC Capital Markets
137
Keeping a Close Eye on Macro Indicators For Consumer Discretionary, Despite Slightly Pricey P/Es
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Has outperformed since early October, but is still middle of the pack vs. most other sectors on that time frame.
▪
Upward EPS have started to improve and now sit in slightly positive territory. Valuations are modestly expensive both on an absolute and 
relative basis. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, the US sector generally captured neutral to negative scores on all of the questions that we asked. Its 
most positive assessment was on valuations, while its most negative assessment was on demand.
▪
After briefly improving, flows to global consumer funds have deteriorated and recently registered the largest weekly outflow since 2022. 
▪
On macro indicators, the sector tends to underperform when interest rates rise. Meanwhile, the sector is one of the most inversely 
correlated to gas/diesel prices, outperforming when prices fall. Not surprisingly, the sector is positively correlated with consumer 
confidence. A turnaround in consumer sentiment would be positive for this sector based on our historical performance analysis, but a 
continuation of interest rate and energy cost pressures would be a headwind.

Consumer Staples Valuations Still Have Room To Run, But Fundamentals Are Complicated
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Consumer Staples has been the best performing sector since early October when the Tech trade peaked, and has stayed strong recently. 
▪
Upward EPS revisions trends have started to improve for the sector and have entered positive territory. Valuations have moved up, but 
are still slightly attractive on a relative basis despite having moved back to neutral on an absolute basis. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, this US sector captured some of the least constructive scores compared to other sectors. Notably, its 
outlook on demand improved from the June survey. The sector scored negatively on performance and the US domestic policy backdrop 
and neutral on valuations. 
▪
After briefly improving, flows to global consumer funds have deteriorated and recently registered the largest weekly outflow since 2022. 
▪
While Health Care is our favorite defensive in a market downdraft, we prefer Staples over Utilities on our list of defensives.
▪
On macro indicators, the sector tends to underperform when interest rates rise. The sector is positively correlated with consumer 
confidence, outperforming as it rises. We don’t find a clear relationship with gas and diesel prices.

On The Sidelines With Energy, Due To Persistent Headline Risk In Both Directions
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Energy has led slightly since early October when the Tech trade peaked but is no longer dominating as it had been late summer/early fall. 
▪
Upward EPS revisions trends have improved recently and are in positive territory but are admittedly closing in on their prior peak. 
Meanwhile, valuations are modestly attractive.
▪
Meanwhile, in our September analyst survey, Energy captured constructive views on performance, valuations, demand, and the domestic 
policy backdrop. The sector was also one of the two that did not get a negative score on our question on the impact of higher rates. 
▪
One problem for the sector has been flows. After a brief return to positive territory, they looked weak in our latest update. 
▪
Our intrigue from our quant work is tempered by its sensitivity to war-related headlines and the limited visibility into how the conflict may 
evolve.  Political winds are blowing against this group given the opposition to the Iran war in the midterms and its perceived pressure on 
oil prices, though the geopolitical community continues to highlight the damage done to the region and difficulty in finding off ramps. 
▪
On macro indicators, tends to lag when consumer confidence rises and gas/diesel prices fall. Outperforms when interest rates rise.

Current
Financials Have Room To Benefit From Leadership Rotation & Economic Resilience
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Financials have outperformed since Tech’s relative performance peak in early October. 
▪
Our September analyst survey captured constructive views on performance, valuations, and demand. While analysts viewed implications 
of higher interest rates negatively, Financials scored considerably less negative on this question than most other sectors. 
▪
While the sector does not appear particularly inexpensive, much of the valuation concern has seemingly subsided, with Financials now 
trading at more reasonable levels – a little above average on absolute P/E and a little below average on relative P/E. 
▪
Revisions trends remain positive on EPS and are improving but have become weaker on sales recently, now sitting in negative territory.
▪
Funds flows had been weak but are improving. 
▪
We are watching capital markets activity with a particular focus on private credit. Improving consumer confidence, which has been 
stabilizing around extreme lows, would be a tailwind if it occurs. We have not yet seen any kind of downgrade to the economic growth 
outlook in the US amid the recent discussion of AI regulation (which some investors worry could contribute to a slowing in capex, which 
has helped support strong US GDP).  We are also watching consumer confidence closely with regards to this sector, as post COVID it 
tends to outperform when consumer confidence rises. Higher interest rates tend to be a headwind, however.

Hiding Out In Health Care, Where Everything Looks Fine On Our Quant Work
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Health Care has outperformed since early October and has continued to trend higher recently, tracking as the second-best performer 
behind Consumer Staples. Overall, defensives are leading the list of top performers. 
▪
Upward EPS revisions remain in positive territory and have improved in recent updates. Valuations are attractive on a relative basis, and 
only very slightly expensive in absolute terms. Flows have been positive in recent months with no signs of slippage. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, the sector captured a positive demand outlook score, but one that was weaker than most other sectors. 
The outlook on performance was slightly positive, but less so than the June survey. Note, our analysts have been more constructive on 
performance at the global level. The US valuation score was also positive in the survey, although not a stand-out across sectors. The 
sector also ranked well relative other sectors in our questions on AI risk and interest rate risk. 
▪
This sector is a natural destination to look to when dialing down Tech exposure. Overall, HC and Tech have performed inversely with one 
another (when looking at both relative to the S&P 500) over the past year. On macro indicators, the sector tends to underperform when 
interest rates rise. Meanwhile, the sector is positively correlated with consumer confidence, outperforming as it rises.

RBC Capital Markets
142
Valuations Remain A Major Challenge For Industrials, When Fundamentals Are Being Questioned
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
A slight outperformer since early October when the Tech trade peaked, but still the 3rd worst performer in the index on that time frame. 
▪
Continues to see positive upward EPS revisions trends. Briefly returned to neutral territory but has bounced. 
▪
Our September analyst survey highlighted constructive views on performance and demand. Assessments of valuations were more neutral 
and views on the domestic policy backdrop were slightly negative.
▪
Fund flows have deteriorated in recent months and are now in negative territory. 
▪
The main concern on this sector is valuation, with Industrials still screening as one of the most expensive sectors on both an absolute and 
relative basis despite weak performance in late summer and early Fall. P/Es have come down but are still well above the long-term 
average.  
▪
We continue to monitor manufacturing activity and the sustainability of AI infrastructure capex spending, the trajectory of which some 
investors are starting to question. Rate hikes also challenge the pricing power of the group, while AI angst challenges the fundamental 
strength narrative more broadly.

Tech is Seeing Deteriorating Revisions & Valuations That Have More Room Left To Fall
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Information Technology has been the worst sector performer since early October. 
▪
Upward EPS estimate revisions are positive but are admittedly slipping from peak-like levels. Valuations have returned to attractive 
territory, but only modestly so. Flows have been strong. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, the US sector captured a positive score for its performance outlook, the state of demand, and the 
domestic policy backdrop, and to a lesser degree, valuations. 
▪
On flows, the trend for Tech funds have been strengthening recently. 
▪
On macro indicators, the sector tends to outperform when interest rates rise but lag when consumer confidence rises. 
▪
The bigger issue for this sector is the path of AI regulation, where strong headwinds have returned post the midterms.

RBC Capital Markets
144
Materials Comes Across As Having a Neutral Profile On Our Work
Source: RBC US Equity Strategy, Bloomberg, S&P Capital IQ / Clarifi
▪
Has outperformed since the Tech trade peaked in early October, but is still middle of the pack vs. other S&P 500 sectors. 
▪
Valuations remain attractive but revisions trends for the sector have deteriorated recently, shifting into negative territory for EPS. 
▪
Fund flows into Materials had stabilized following a stretch of weakness earlier in the year, but never really took off again. The sector 
recently registered its largest weekly outflow since 2022. 
▪
In our September analyst survey, the sector scored constructively on performance and valuations, though views on demand were 
negative. Materials was also among the sectors that captured a negative score on our interest rate risk question. 
▪
We continue to monitor commodity price trends and the durability of infrastructure and reshoring-related spending.
▪
Would benefit from an improvement in some of the macro indicators we’re keeping a close eye on. Relative performance tends to move 
inversely with interest rates and in sync with consumer confidence, meaning it should outperform if confidence rises and rates fall, but 
underperform if they do the opposite.
